Appendix A
Supplementary Methods
47
A.1
Template-Based AI Scientist Implementation Details
48
This section outlines the implementation details for the template-based version of The
49
AI Scientist. This version is built upon Large Language Models (LLMs), which are
50
embedded into agentic frameworks to enhance their reasoning and coding capabilities.
51
A.1.1
Prompts for Template-Based Scientific Discovery
52
The template-based version of The AI Scientist uses structured prompts to guide
53
Large Language Models (LLMs) through the research stages from idea generation to
54
paper writing.
55
The process begins by setting the overall goal for The AI Scientist.
56
Idea Generation System Prompt
You are an ambitious AI PhD student who is looking to publish a
paper that will contribute significantly to the field.
57
The AI Scientist is then prompted to generate a specific research idea which
58
includes an experimental plan, and self-assessed scores on novelty, feasibility, and
59
interestingness.
60
Idea Generation Prompt
{task_description}
<experiment.py>
{code}
</experiment.py>
Here are the ideas that you have already generated:
'''
{prev_ideas_string}
'''
Come up with the next impactful and creative idea for research
experiments and directions you can feasibly investigate with the code
provided. Note that you will not have access to any additional resources
or datasets. Make sure any idea is not overfit the specific training
dataset or model, and has wider significance.
Respond in the following format:
THOUGHT:
<THOUGHT>
61
3


NEW IDEA JSON:
```json
<JSON>
```
In <THOUGHT>, first briefly discuss your intuitions and motivations for
the idea. Detail your high-level plan, necessary design choices and
ideal outcomes of the experiments. Justify how the idea is different
from the existing ones.
In <JSON>, provide the new idea in JSON format with the following
fields:
- "Name": A shortened descriptor of the idea. Lowercase, no spaces,
underscores allowed.
- "Title": A title for the idea, will be used for the report writing.
- "Experiment": An outline of the implementation. E.g. which functions
need to be added or modified, how results will be obtained, ...
- "Interestingness": A rating from 1 to 10 (lowest to highest).
- "Feasibility": A rating from 1 to 10 (lowest to highest).
- "Novelty": A rating from 1 to 10 (lowest to highest).
Be cautious and realistic on your ratings.
This JSON will be automatically parsed, so ensure the format is precise.
You will have {num_reflections} rounds to iterate on the idea, but do
not need to use them all.
62
To assess the novelty of the generated idea, The AI Scientist is instructed to use
63
the Semantic Scholar API to search the literature.
64
Idea Novelty System Prompt
You are an ambitious AI PhD student who is looking to publish a paper
that will contribute significantly to the field.
You have an idea and you want to check if it is novel or not. I.e., not
overlapping significantly with existing literature or already well
explored.
Be a harsh critic for novelty, ensure there is a sufficient contribution
in the idea for a new conference or workshop paper.
You will be given access to the Semantic Scholar API, which you may use
to survey the literature and find relevant papers to help you make your
decision.
The top 10 results for any search query will be presented to you with
the abstracts.
You will be given {num_rounds} to decide on the paper, but you do not
need to use them all.
At any round, you may exit early and decide on the novelty of the idea.
65
4


Decide a paper idea is novel if after sufficient searching, you have not
found a paper that significantly overlaps with your idea.
Decide a paper idea is not novel, if you have found a paper that
significantly overlaps with your idea.
{task_description}
<experiment.py>
{code}
</experiment.py>
66
The novelty check proceeds iteratively, allowing multiple queries.
67
Idea Novelty Prompt
Round {current_round}/{num_rounds}.
You have this idea:
"""
{idea}
"""
The results of the last query are (empty on first round):
"""
{last_query_results}
"""
Respond in the following format:
THOUGHT:
<THOUGHT>
RESPONSE:
```json
<JSON>
```
In <THOUGHT>, first briefly reason over the idea and identify any query
that could help you make your decision.
If you have made your decision, add "Decision made: novel." or "Decision
made: not novel." to your thoughts.
In <JSON>, respond in JSON format with ONLY the following field:
- "Query": An optional search query to search the literature (e.g.
attention is all you need). You must make a query if you have not
decided this round.
68
5


A query will work best if you are able to recall the exact name of the
paper you are looking for, or the authors.
This JSON will be automatically parsed, so ensure the format is precise.
69
For experiment implementation and execution, The AI Scientist uses the state-of-
70
the-art code editor Aider.
71
Experiment Running Aider Prompt
Your goal is to implement the following idea: {title}.
The proposed experiment is as follows: {idea}.
You are given a total of up to {max_runs} runs to complete the necessary
experiments. You do not need to use all {max_runs}.
First, plan the list of experiments you would like to run. For example,
if you are sweeping over a specific hyperparameter, plan each value you
would like to test for each run.
Note that we already provide the vanilla baseline results, so you do not
need to re-run it.
For reference, the baseline results are as follows:
{baseline_results}
After you complete each change, we will run the command `python
experiment.py --out_dir=run_i' where i is the run number and evaluate
the results.
YOUR PROPOSED CHANGE MUST USE THIS COMMAND FORMAT, DO NOT ADD ADDITIONAL
COMMAND LINE ARGS.
You can then implement the next thing on your list.
72
After experiments, The AI Scientist uses Aider to generate plots from the results
73
and records experimental details.
74
Plotting Aider Prompt
Great job! Please modify `plot.py` to generate the most relevant plots
for the final writeup.
In particular, be sure to fill in the "labels" dictionary with the
correct names for each run that you want to plot.
Only the runs in the `labels` dictionary will be plotted, so make sure
to include all relevant runs.
We will be running the command `python plot.py` to generate the plots.
75
6


---
Please modify `notes.txt` with a description of what each plot shows
along with the filename of the figure. Please do so in-depth.
Somebody else will be using `notes.txt` to write a report on this in the
future.
76
For manuscript writing, The AI Scientist uses Aider to fill a LaTeX template
77
section by section with the produced plots and results.
78
Paper Writing Aider Prompt
We've provided the `latex/template.tex` file to the project. We will be
filling it in section by section.
First, please fill in the {section} section of the writeup.
Some tips are provided below:
{per_section_tips}
Before every paragraph, please include a brief description of what you
plan to write in that paragraph in a comment.
Be sure to first name the file and use *SEARCH/REPLACE* blocks to
perform these edits.
79

81
Additional templates are available in our GitHub repository: https://github.com/

SakanaAI/AI-Scientist/tree/main/templates.

Generalized Idea Generation System and User Prompt
# This file trains a DDPM diffusion model on 2D datasets.
import argparse
import json
import os.path as osp
import pathlib
import pickle
import time
import npeet.entropy_estimators as ee
import numpy as np
84
7


import torch
from torch import nn
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch.optim.lr_scheduler import CosineAnnealingLR
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
from tqdm.auto import tqdm
import datasets
from ema_pytorch import EMA
device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
class SinusoidalEmbedding(nn.Module):
def __init__(self, dim: int, scale: float = 1.0):
super().__init__()
self.dim = dim
self.scale = scale
def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor):
x = x * self.scale
half_dim = self.dim // 2
emb = torch.log(torch.Tensor([10000.0])) / (half_dim - 1)
emb = torch.exp(-emb * torch.arange(half_dim)).to(device)
emb = x.unsqueeze(-1) * emb.unsqueeze(0)
emb = torch.cat((torch.sin(emb), torch.cos(emb)), dim=-1)
return emb
class ResidualBlock(nn.Module):
def __init__(self, width: int):
super().__init__()
self.ff = nn.Linear(width, width)
self.act = nn.ReLU()
def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor):
return x + self.ff(self.act(x))
class MLPDenoiser(nn.Module):
def __init__(
self,
embedding_dim: int = 128,
hidden_dim: int = 256,
hidden_layers: int = 3,
):
super().__init__()
self.time_mlp = SinusoidalEmbedding(embedding_dim)
85
8


# sinusoidal embeddings help capture high-frequency patterns for
low-dim data
self.input_mlp1 = SinusoidalEmbedding(eAs with human-authored
research, some AI-generated ideambedding_dim, scale=25.0)
self.input_mlp2 = SinusoidalEmbedding(embedding_dim, scale=25.0)
self.network = nn.Sequential(
nn.Linear(embedding_dim * 3, hidden_dim),
*[ResidualBlock(hidden_dim) for _ in range(hidden_layers)],
nn.ReLU(),
nn.Linear(hidden_dim, 2),
)
def forward(self, x, t):
x1_emb = self.input_mlp1(x[:, 0])
x2_emb = self.input_mlp2(x[:, 1])
t_emb = self.time_mlp(t)
emb = torch.cat([x1_emb, x2_emb, t_emb], dim=-1)
return self.network(emb)
class NoiseScheduler():
def __init__(
self,
num_timesteps=1000,
beta_start=0.0001,
beta_end=0.02,
beta_schedule="linear",
):
self.num_timesteps = num_timesteps
if beta_schedule == "linear":
self.betas = torch.linspace(
beta_start, beta_end, num_timesteps,
dtype=torch.float32).to(device)
elif beta_schedule == "quadratic":
self.betas = (torch.linspace(
beta_start ** 0.5, beta_end ** 0.5, num_timesteps,
dtype=torch.float32) ** 2).to(device)
else:
raise ValueError(f"Unknown beta schedule: {beta_schedule}")
self.alphas = 1.0 - self.betas
self.alphas_cumprod = torch.cumprod(self.alphas,
axis=0).to(device)
self.alphas_cumprod_prev = F.pad(self.alphas_cumprod[:-1], (1,
0), value=1.).to(device)
# required for self.add_noise
86
9


self.sqrt_alphas_cumprod = (self.alphas_cumprod **
0.5).to(device)
self.sqrt_one_minus_alphas_cumprod = ((1 - self.alphas_cumprod)
** 0.5).to(device)
# required for reconstruct_x0
self.sqrt_inv_alphas_cumprod = torch.sqrt(1 /
self.alphas_cumprod).to(device)
self.sqrt_inv_alphas_cumprod_minus_one = torch.sqrt(
1 / self.alphas_cumprod - 1).to(device)
# required for q_posterior
self.posterior_mean_coef1 = self.betas *
torch.sqrt(self.alphas_cumprod_prev) / (1. -
self.alphas_cumprod).to(
device)
self.posterior_mean_coef2 = ((1. - self.alphas_cumprod_prev) *
torch.sqrt(self.alphas) / (
1. - self.alphas_cumprod)).to(device)
def reconstruct_x0(self, x_t, t, noise):
s1 = self.sqrt_inv_alphas_cumprod[t]
s2 = self.sqrt_inv_alphas_cumprod_minus_one[t]
s1 = s1.reshape(-1, 1)
s2 = s2.reshape(-1, 1)
return s1 * x_t - s2 * noise
def q_posterior(self, x_0, x_t, t):
s1 = self.posterior_mean_coef1[t]
s2 = self.posterior_mean_coef2[t]
s1 = s1.reshape(-1, 1)
s2 = s2.reshape(-1, 1)
mu = s1 * x_0 + s2 * x_t
return mu
def get_variance(self, t):
if t == 0:
return 0
variance = self.betas[t] * (1. - self.alphas_cumprod_prev[t]) /
(1. - self.alphas_cumprod[t])
variance = variance.clip(1e-20)
return variance
def step(self, model_output, timestep, sample):
t = timestep
pred_original_sample = self.reconstruct_x0(sample, t,
model_output)
87
10


pred_prev_sample = self.q_posterior(pred_original_sample,
sample, t)
variance = 0
if t > 0:
noise = torch.randn_like(model_output)
variance = (self.get_variance(t) ** 0.5) * noise
pred_prev_sample = pred_prev_sample + variance
return pred_prev_sample
def add_noise(self, x_start, x_noise, timesteps):
s1 = self.sqrt_alphas_cumprod[timesteps]
s2 = self.sqrt_one_minus_alphas_cumprod[timesteps]
s1 = s1.reshape(-1, 1)
s2 = s2.reshape(-1, 1)
return s1 * x_start + s2 * x_noise
def __len__(self):
return self.num_timesteps
if __name__ == "__main__":
parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()
parser.add_argument("--train_batch_size", type=int, default=256)
parser.add_argument("--eval_batch_size", type=int, default=10000)
parser.add_argument("--learning_rate", type=float, default=3e-4)
parser.add_argument("--num_timesteps", type=int, default=100)
parser.add_argument("--num_train_steps", type=int, default=10000)
parser.add_argument("--beta_schedule", type=str, default="linear",
choices=["linear", "quadratic"])
parser.add_argument("--embedding_dim", type=int, default=128)
parser.add_argument("--hidden_size", type=int, default=256)
parser.add_argument("--hidden_layers", type=int, default=3)
parser.add_argument("--out_dir", type=str, default="run_0")
config = parser.parse_args()
final_infos = {}
all_results = {}
pathlib.Path(config.out_dir).mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
for dataset_name in ["circle", "dino", "line", "moons"]:
dataset = datasets.get_dataset(dataset_name, n=100000)
88
11


dataloader = DataLoader(dataset,
batch_size=config.train_batch_size, shuffle=True)
model = MLPDenoiser(
embedding_dim=config.embedding_dim,
hidden_dim=config.hidden_size,
hidden_layers=config.hidden_layers,
).to(device)
ema_model = EMA(model, beta=0.995, update_every=10).to(device)
noise_scheduler =
NoiseScheduler(num_timesteps=config.num_timesteps,
beta_schedule=config.beta_schedule)
optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(
model.parameters(),
lr=config.learning_rate,
)
scheduler = CosineAnnealingLR(optimizer,
T_max=config.num_train_steps)
train_losses = []
print("Training model...")
model.train()
global_step = 0
progress_bar = tqdm(total=config.num_train_steps,
mininterval=10, disable=True)
progress_bar.set_description("Training")
start_time = time.time()
while global_step < config.num_train_steps:
for batch in dataloader:
if global_step >= config.num_train_steps:
break
batch = batch[0].to(device)
noise = torch.randn(batch.shape).to(device)
timesteps = torch.randint(
0, noise_scheduler.num_timesteps, (batch.shape[0],)
).long().to(device)
noisy = noise_scheduler.add_noise(batch, noise,
timesteps)
noise_pred = model(noisy, timesteps)
loss = F.mse_loss(noise_pred, noise)
loss.backward()
nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), 0.5)
optimizer.step()
89
12


optimizer.zero_grad()
ema_model.update()
scheduler.step()
progress_bar.update(1)
logs = {"loss": loss.detach().item()}
train_losses.append(loss.detach().item())
progress_bar.set_postfix(**logs)
global_step += 1
progress_bar.close()
end_time = time.time()
training_time = end_time - start_time
# Eval loss
model.eval()
eval_losses = []
for batch in dataloader:
batch = batch[0].to(device)
noise = torch.randn(batch.shape).to(device)
timesteps = torch.randint(
0, noise_scheduler.num_timesteps, (batch.shape[0],)
).long().to(device)
noisy = noise_scheduler.add_noise(batch, noise, timesteps)
noise_pred = model(noisy, timesteps)
loss = F.mse_loss(noise_pred, noise)
eval_losses.append(loss.detach().item())
eval_loss = np.mean(eval_losses)
# Eval image saving
ema_model.eval()
sample = torch.randn(config.eval_batch_size, 2).to(device)
timesteps = list(range(len(noise_scheduler)))[::-1]
inference_start_time = time.time()
for t in timesteps:
t = torch.from_numpy(np.repeat(t,
config.eval_batch_size)).long().to(device)
with torch.no_grad():
residual = ema_model(sample, t)
sample = noise_scheduler.step(residual, t[0], sample)
sample = sample.cpu().numpy()
inference_end_time = time.time()
inference_time = inference_end_time - inference_start_time
# Eval estimated KL
real_data = dataset.tensors[0].numpy()
kl_divergence = ee.kldiv(real_data, sample, k=5)
90
13


final_infos[dataset_name] = {
"means": {
"training_time": training_time,
"eval_loss": eval_loss,
"inference_time": inference_time,
"kl_divergence": kl_divergence,
}
}
all_results[dataset_name] = {
"train_losses": train_losses,
"images": sample,
}
with open(osp.join(config.out_dir, "final_info.json"), "w") as f:
json.dump(final_infos, f)
with open(osp.join(config.out_dir, "all_results.pkl"), "wb") as f:
pickle.dump(all_results, f)

A.2
Template-Free AI Scientist Implementation Details
95
This section outlines the implementation details for the more open-ended template-
96
free version of The AI Scientist, as described in the main text. This enhanced version
97
operates without requiring human-authored code templates, instead employing gen-
98
eralized idea generation, agentic tree search for experimentation, and integrated
99
Vision-Language Model (VLM) feedback, building upon the foundational capabilities
100
of the template-based version of The AI Scientist.
101
14


A.2.1
System Architecture and Execution Flow
102
The AI Scientist workflow consists of multiple sequential stages, from ideation to
103
experimentation to manuscript generation. The transitions across these stages are
104
fully automated, requiring no human intervention. A single driver process orchestrates
105
the pipeline: it generates candidate programs via LLM calls, launches experiments,
106
monitors exit codes, logs, and timeouts, and triggers state transitions according to
107
pre-defined criteria. Specifically:
108
• Stage 1 →Stage 2: Transition occurs once the system produces runnable code
109
with no runtime errors.
110
• Stage 2 →Stage 3: Triggered when the generated code outperforms the base-
111
line model under pre-defined metrics (which are themselves defined by the LLM
112
immediately after ideation).
113
• Stage 3 →Stage 4: Initiated automatically once the Stage 3 tree search spends
114
its allocated exploration budget.
115
Each experiment is executed via Python’s non-interactive subprocess module, with
116
all interactions between the generated code and the operating system limited to con-
117
trolled file I/O within per-run working directories. After each run, artifacts such as
118
metrics, plots, runtime logs, and VLM feedback are serialized into a typed Python
119
object representing a tree node. The driver process then invokes an LLM-based judge
120
to evaluate and select the best node from the current stage to carry forward. All tool
121
invocations (code generation, analysis, or VLM feedback) are made through internal
122
Python-callable interfaces that wrap the respective LLM API calls.
123
A.2.2
Cross-Stage Consistency
124
Each stage exports its best node to the following stage, which then uses it to guide
125
subsequent generation. The transition from ideation to experimentation is managed
126
by passing a finalized idea JSON that encodes the proposed hypothesis, task setup,
127
and evaluation metrics. The transition from experimentation to manuscript writing
128
is handled by passing a condensed form of the experiment journal summarizing key
129
results and observations, where the summarization is done by an LLM.
130
This mechanism grounds each later stage in the outputs of earlier ones, mitigating
131
divergence between the initial proposal, results, and final write-up.
132
A.2.3
Model Selection per Stage
133
Different stages demand distinct capabilities from the underlying models. In our cur-
134
rent implementation, we empirically identified the most effective model for each role
135
through small-scale pilot runs—for example, Claude for code generation, and GPT-
136
series models for high-level planning, analysis, and writing. While future foundation
137
models may unify these capabilities, the system is designed to flexibly substitute
138
models per stage without altering the overall architecture.
139
15


A.2.4
Experimental Journal Structure
140
In the template-based setting, the journal is a plain-text log summarizing experimental
141
attempts. In the template-free setting, it is implemented as a Python class producing a
142
structured JSON export, where each tree node records the following fields: - Generated
143
code and execution plan - Experimental results (metrics, losses, validation scores, etc.)
144
- Runtime feedback, including error traces or log summaries - Vision-Language Model
145
(VLM) commentary on generated figures or visual outputs - LLM judge evaluations
146
and stage transition signals
147
This structured journal not only ensures reproducibility and auditability across
148
experiments but also provides a machine-readable record that connects all stages of
149
the automated research process.
150
A.2.5
Comparison with Deep Research
151
Recent systems such as Deep Research1 represent major progress in large-scale
152
reasoning-based information synthesis. They are designed to retrieve, analyze, and
153
summarize existing information to produce well-researched answers to a user’s query.
154
However, these systems remain limited to knowledge aggregation, and they cannot
155
design or execute new experiments.
156
The AI Scientist, in contrast, automates the entire research lifecycle involved in
157
producing a single paper. It begins with ideation, proceeds through experimental
158
implementation and evaluation, and culminates in writing full scientific manuscripts.
159
This capability moves beyond retrieval and synthesis toward autonomous knowledge
160
creation, positioning The AI Scientist as a complementary but distinct class of system
161
from Deep Research tools.
162
A.2.6
Prompts for Template-Free Scientific Discovery
163
The template-free version of The AI Scientist utilizes structured prompts to guide
164
Large Language Models (LLMs) through its more autonomous research cycle. This
165
includes a more generalized idea generation phase, a sophisticated experiment design
166
and execution process, and manuscript writing enhanced by VLM-assisted refinement
167
(see main text).
168
The idea generation process begins at a higher level of abstraction than the
169
template-based version. The template-free system is prompted for open-ended think-
170
ing, akin to formulating a research abstract, while integrating literature search
171
tools.
172
16

method

loop: `batch = {k: v.to(device) for k, v in batch.items() if

Plot Aggregation Prompt
# System prompt
You are an ambitious AI researcher who is preparing final plots for a
scientific paper submission.
You have multiple experiment summaries (baseline, research, ablation),
each possibly containing references to different plots or numerical
insights.
There is also a top-level 'research_idea.md' file that outlines the
overarching research direction.
Your job is to produce ONE Python script that fully aggregates and
visualizes the final results for a comprehensive research paper.
Key points:
1) Combine or replicate relevant existing plotting code, referencing how
data was originally generated (from code references) to ensure
correctness.
2) Create a complete set of final scientific plots, stored in 'figures/'
only (since only those are used in the final paper).
3) Make sure to use existing .npy data for analysis; do NOT hallucinate
data. If single numeric results are needed, these may be copied from the
JSON summaries.
4) Only create plots where the data is best presented as a figure and
not as a table. E.g. don't use bar plots if the data is hard to visually
compare.
5) The final aggregator script must be in triple backticks and stand
alone so it can be dropped into a codebase and run.
6) If there are plots based on synthetic data, include them in the
appendix.
Implement best practices:
- Do not produce extraneous or irrelevant plots.
- Maintain clarity, minimal but sufficient code.
- Demonstrate thoroughness for a final research paper submission.
- Do NOT reference non-existent files or images.
- Use the .npy files to get data for the plots and key numbers from the
JSON summaries.
- Demarcate each individual plot, and put them in separate try-catch
blocks so that the failure of one plot does not affect the others.
189
25


- Make sure to only create plots that are unique and needed for the
final paper and appendix. A good number could be around {MAX_FIGURES}
plots in total.
- Aim to aggregate multiple figures into one plot if suitable, i.e. if
they are all related to the same topic. You can place up to 3 plots in
one row.
- Provide well-labeled plots (axes, legends, titles) that highlight main
findings. Use informative names everywhere, including in the legend for
referencing them in the final paper. Make sure the legend is always
visible.
- Make the plots look professional (if applicable, no top and right
spines, dpi of 300, adequate ylim, etc.).
- Do not use labels with underscores, e.g. "loss_vs_epoch" should be
"loss vs epoch".
- For image examples, select a few categories/classes to showcase the
diversity of results instead of showing a single category/class. Some
can be included in the main paper, while the rest can go in the
appendix.
Your output should be the entire Python aggregator script in triple
backticks.

Manuscript Writing Prompt (ICBINB workshop specific)
# System prompt
You are an ambitious AI researcher who is looking to publish a paper to
the "I Can't Believe It's Not Better" (ICBINB) Workshop at ICLR 2025.
This workshop aims to highlight real-world pitfalls, challenges, and
negative or inconclusive results in deep learning, encouraging open
discussion.
You must accurately represent the results of the experiments.
The main paper is limited to {page_limit} pages in single-column format,
not counting references. In general, try to use the available space and
include all relevant information.
195
27


DO NOT USE MORE THAN {page_limit} PAGES FOR THE MAIN TEXT. MINIMIZE THE
USAGE OF ITEMIZE OR ENUMERATE. ONLY USE THEM IF THEY ARE ABSOLUTELY
NECESSARY AND CONTAIN SUBSTANTIAL INFORMATION.
Ensure that the tables and figures are correctly placed in a reasonable
location and format.
- Do not change the overall style which is mandated by the conference.
Keep to the current method of including the references.bib file.
- Do not remove the \\graphicspath directive or no figures will be
found.
- Do not add `Acknowledgements` section to the paper.
Here are some tips for each section of the paper:
- **Title**:
- Title should be catchy and informative. It should give a good idea
of what the paper is about.
- Try to keep it under 2 lines.
- **Abstract**:
- Brief summary highlighting the nature of the challenge or pitfall
explored.
- Concise motivation of why this matters for real-world deployment.
- This should be one continuous paragraph.
- **Introduction**:
- Overview of the issue or challenge being explored.
- Clearly state why this problem is important, especially for
practical or real-world contexts.
- Summarize your contributions or findings: they may include negative
results, real-world pitfalls, unexpected behaviors, or partial
improvements.
- **Related Work**:
- Cite relevant papers or approaches that have tackled similar issues
or have encountered similar pitfalls.
- Compare and contrast with your own findings.
- **Background** (optional):
- Provide necessary technical or domain-specific background if needed.
- **Method / Problem Discussion**:
- Detail the problem context or the method if it is relevant to
highlight the challenges faced.
- If results are not strictly an improvement, discuss partial
successes or lessons learned.
- **Experiments** (if applicable):
196
28


- Present results truthfully according to the data you have. Negative,
unexpected, or inconclusive findings are valid contributions for this
workshop.
- Include figures, tables, or real-world examples that illustrate the
pitfalls.
- Include up to 4 figures in the main text. All other figures should
be in the appendix.
- **Conclusion**:
- Summarize the main lessons learned or contributions.
- Suggest next steps or future directions, highlighting how these
insights can help the community avoid or overcome similar issues.
- **Appendix**:
- Place for supplementary material that did not fit in the main paper.
- Add more information and details (hyperparameters, algorithms, etc.)
in the supplementary material.
- Add more plots and tables in the supplementary material. Make sure
that this information is not already covered in the main paper.
- When checking for duplicate figures, be sure to also review their
descriptions to catch cases where different figures convey the same
information. For example, one figure might present aggregated training
accuracy as a single line plot with a shaded standard deviation (e.g.,
aggregated_training_accuracy.png), while another
(per_seed_training_accuracy.png) shows the same data as three separate
line plots.
Ensure you are always writing good compilable LaTeX code.
Common mistakes that should be fixed include:
- LaTeX syntax errors (unenclosed math, unmatched braces, etc.).
- Duplicate figure labels or references.
- Unescaped special characters: & % $ # _ {{ }} ˜ ˆ \\
- Proper table/figure closure.
- Do not hallucinate new citations or any results not in the logs.
Ensure proper citation usage:
- Always include references within
\begin{{filecontents}}{{references.bib}} ... \end{{filecontents}}, even
if they haven't changed from the previous round.
- Use citations from the provided references.bib content.
- Each section (especially Related Work) should have multiple citations.
When returning final code, place it in fenced triple backticks with
'latex' syntax highlighting.

You will be given an image via the vision API. As a careful scientist
reviewer, your task is to:
1. Examine the provided image closely.
2. Describe in detail what the image shows in a scientific manner.
3. Critically analyze whether the image content aligns with the given
caption:
{caption}
4. We also have references in the main text that mention the figure:
{main_text_figrefs}
You should:
- Examine the figure in detail: conclude elements in figures (e.g.,
name of axis) and describe what information is shown (e.g,. the line
of loss decrease monotonically but plateau after X epoch)
- Suggest any potential improvements or issues in the figure itself
(e.g., missing legend, unclear labeling, no meaningful conclusion,
mismatch with what the caption claims).
- Critique the caption: does it accurately describe the figure? Is it
too long/short? Does it include a concise takeaway?
- Review how well the main text references (figrefs) explain the
figure:
Are they missing? Do they adequately describe the figure's content,
context, or purpose?
Finally, respond in the following format:
THOUGHT:
<THOUGHT>
REVIEW JSON:
```json
<JSON>
```
In <JSON>, provide the review in JSON format with the following fields
in the order:
- "Img_description": "<Describe the figure's contents here>"
- "Img_review": "<Your analysis of the figure itself, including any
suggestions for improvement>"
203
32


- "Caption_review": "<Your assessment of how well the caption matches
the figure and any suggestions>"
- "Figrefs_review": "<Your thoughts on whether the main text references
adequately describe or integrate the figure>"
In <THOUGHT>, first, thoroughly reason through your observations,
analysis of alignment, and any suggested improvements. It is okay to be
very long. Then, provide your final structured output in <JSON>. Make
sure the JSON is valid and properly formatted, as it will be parsed
automatically.

VLMs also assist in a broader reflection on figure selection and placement during
205
manuscript refinement.
206
VLM-Assisted Manuscript Figure Reflection Prompt
The following figures are currently used in the paper:
{sorted(used_figs)}
The following figures are available in the folder but not used in the
LaTeX: {sorted(unused_figs)}
{reflection_page_info}
The following is the VLM review on figures:
{review_img_selection}
Please review the figures and make the following changes:
1. For figures that do not add significant value to the paper, move them
to the appendix
2. For figures that are not very informative or do not effectively
communicate meaningful patterns, remove them entirely
3. For figures that do not contain subfigures and present sparse
information, consider combining them with other related figures
4. Update all relevant text discussions to reflect any changes in figure
placement or combination
5. Enhance the scientific analysis of the remaining figures in the text
- provide detailed, insightful discussions of their significance and
findings
Please ensure all changes maintain scientific rigor and improve the
paper's clarity and impact. Be more aggressive with figure selection -
move more figures to the
appendix or group them together with other
figures if the page limit is already exceeded.
If you believe you are done with reflection, simply say: "I am done".

A.2.7
Example idea generated for the ICLR ICBINB workshop
212
experiment
213
Example idea generated for the ICLR ICBINB workshop experiment
"Name": "compositional_regularization_nn",
"Title": "Enhancing Compositional Generalization in Neural Networks via
Compositional Regularization",
"Short Hypothesis": "Introducing a compositional regularization term
during training can encourage neural networks to develop compositional
representations, thereby improving their ability to generalize to novel
combinations of known components.",
"Related Work": "Previous work has highlighted the challenges neural
networks face in achieving compositional generalization. Studies such as
'Compositional Generalization through Abstract Representations in Human
and Artificial Neural Networks' (Ito et al., NeurIPS 2022) have explored
abstract representations to tackle this issue. However, limited research
focuses on directly incorporating explicit regularization terms into the
training objective to enforce compositional structures. Our proposal
distinguishes itself by introducing a novel regularization approach that
penalizes deviations from predefined compositional patterns during
training, encouraging the network to internalize compositional rules.",
214
35


"Abstract": "Neural networks excel in many tasks but often struggle with
compositional generalization--the ability to understand and generate
novel combinations of familiar components. This limitation hampers their
performance on tasks requiring systematic generalization beyond the
training data. In this proposal, we introduce a novel training method
that incorporates an explicit compositional regularization term into the
loss function of neural networks. This regularization term is designed
to encourage the formation of compositional representations by
penalizing the network when its internal representations deviate from
expected compositional structures. We hypothesize that this approach
will enhance the network's ability to generalize to unseen combinations,
mimicking human-like compositional reasoning. We will test our method on
synthetic benchmarks like the SCAN and COGS datasets, which are
specifically designed to evaluate compositional generalization, as well
as on real-world tasks such as machine translation and semantic parsing.
By comparing our method to baseline models and existing approaches, we
aim to demonstrate significant improvements in generalization
performance. This work offers a new avenue for enforcing
compositionality in neural networks through regularization, potentially
bridging the gap between neural network capabilities and human cognitive
flexibility.",
"Experiments": [
"Implement the compositional regularization term and integrate it
into the loss function of standard sequence-to-sequence neural
network architectures with attention mechanisms.",
"Train models on synthetic datasets like SCAN and COGS, evaluating
performance on compositional generalization tasks with and without
the regularization term.",
"Apply the method to real-world tasks such as machine translation
using the IWSLT dataset and semantic parsing with the GeoQuery
dataset, assessing improvements in generalization to new language
constructs.",
"Analyze the learned representations by visualizing embedding spaces
and utilizing compositionality metrics to assess how the
regularization affects internal representations.",
"Conduct ablation studies to determine the impact of different
strengths of the regularization term, identifying the optimal
balance between enforcing compositionality and maintaining overall
performance.",
"Compare the proposed method against other approaches aimed at
improving compositional generalization, such as meta-learning
techniques and specialized architectures."
],
"Risk Factors and Limitations": [
"The effectiveness of the compositional regularization may vary
across different datasets and tasks, potentially limiting its
generalizability.",
215
36


"An improperly balanced regularization term could negatively impact
model performance on the primary task, leading to lower accuracy.",
"Additional computational overhead from calculating the
regularization term may increase training time and resource
requirements.",
"Defining appropriate compositional structures for complex or
less-understood domains may be challenging, affecting the
applicability of the method.",
"The approach may face scalability issues when applied to very large
models or datasets common in industrial applications."]
216
A.2.8
Example Deliverables by Stage (Template-Free Run)
217
The research pipeline comprises four stages: a working baseline is first implemented
218
(Stage 1), then refined via hyperparameter tuning (Stage 2). The resulting code
219
seeds the main research exploration (Stage 3), followed by systematic ablation studies
220
(Stage 4). In this section, we present example outputs for each stage. To save space,
221
we omit generated code and show only one of ten Stage 4 ablation results.
222
Idea. Attribute–Jacobian Regularization (AJR) penalizes a model’s sensitivity
223
to a differentiable augmentation parameter α that controls a suspected spurious
224
attribute, encouraging predictions to be invariant to that attribute without requiring
225
group labels.
226
Stage 1. A minimal Colored-MNIST demo applies AJR as a penalty on the
227
loss’s sensitivity to a per-example color-mixing coefficient α (interpolating image ↔
228
grayscale) and evaluates robustness via worst-group accuracy over (label, color) pairs
229
(20 groups).
230
Stage 2. The same AJR formulation is used while sweeping hyperparameters
231
(learning rate, batch size, epochs) on both Colored-MNIST and Colored-Fashion-
232
MNIST.
233
Stage 3. An “aligned AJR” variant precomputes two spurious “views” per image
234
and interpolates along the exact spurious axis; the system also adds CIFAR-10 with
235
warm/cool tints, employs a stronger CNN with weight decay, a learning-rate scheduler,
236
and gradient clipping, and reports overall accuracy, worst-group accuracy, and the
237
spurious-robust gap.
238
Stage 4. An ablation study varies the strength of the spurious factor during
239
training via an α-schedule, analyzing how schedule design affects robustness.
240

The total time required for the template-free version of The AI Scientist to generate
269
a single paper depends on the complexity of the problems. Based on our experience,
270
this process usually takes anywhere from several hours to a maximum of 15 hours,
271
which is the runtime limit we have set.
272
For Figure 1(B), the following models are used: ‘gpt-4-0613’, ‘gpt-4o-2024-05-13’,
273
‘o1-2024-12-17’, ‘claude-3-sonnet-20240229-v1’, ‘claude-sonnet-4-20250514-v1’, ‘gpt-
274
3.5-turbo’, ‘gemini-2.0-flash’, ‘gemini-2.5-pro-preview-06-05’, ‘o3-2025-04-16’, ‘claude-
275
3-5-sonnet-20241022-v2’, and ‘gemini-1.5-pro’. Each data point for the template-free
276
runs in the graph represents the mean and standard error calculated over two write-ups
277
for each of three different ideas, totaling six papers. Each data point for the template-
278
based runs represents the mean and standard error calculated over one writeup for
279
each of three different ideas, totaling three papers.
280
For Figure 3(C), ‘claude-sonnet-4-20250514-v1’ is used, and several total node
281
budgets are tested: 32 (Stage 3: 16, Stage 4: 16), 16 (Stage 3: 8, Stage 4: 8), 8 (Stage
282
3: 4, Stage 4: 4), and 4 (Stage 3: 2, Stage 4: 2). To isolate the impact of different
283
60


experiment runs, checkpoints are saved after Stage 3 and Stage 4 is re-run multiple
284
times. In each run, only the first k nodes from Stage 3 are considered (for k ∈{2, 4, 8}),
285
the best node among them is selected, and Stage 4 is initialized from that node, using
286
a Stage 4 node budget of k. For each node setting, 6 papers are generated (2 papers
287
for each of 3 ideas). The Automated Reviewer is then run 5 times on each paper,
288
resulting in 30 samples per data point. The mean and standard error across these 30
289
samples are plotted.
290
A.3
The Automated Reviewer Details
291
A.3.1
Prompts for Automated Paper Reviewing
292
An Automated Reviewer component evaluates the generated manuscripts. This com-
293
ponent was designed to mimic the peer-review process at a major machine learning
294
conference, using guidelines from NeurIPS. The full validation of this component is
295
described in Section A.3.2.
296
The reviewer LLM is first given instructions for its task.
297
Paper Review System Prompt
You are an AI researcher who is reviewing a paper that was
submitted to a prestigious ML venue.
298
The main review prompt provides the paper, guidelines from the NeurIPS reviewer
299
guidelines, and asks for a structured review.
300
Paper Review Prompt
## Review Form
Below is a description of the questions you will be asked on the review
form for each paper and some guidelines on what to consider when
answering these questions.
When writing your review, please keep in mind that after decisions have
been made, reviews and meta-reviews of accepted papers and opted-in
rejected papers will be made public.
{neurips_reviewer_guidelines}
{few_shot_examples_of_human_reviews}
Here is the paper you are asked to review:
```
{paper}
```
301
Multiple reviews are generated for each paper and aggregated into a single meta-
302
review, with an LLM taking the role of the Area Chair.
303
61


Paper Review Ensembling System Prompt
You are an Area Chair at a machine learning conference.
You are in charge of meta-reviewing a paper that was reviewed by
{reviewer_count} reviewers.
Your job is to aggregate the reviews into a single meta-review in the
same format.
Be critical and cautious in your decision, find consensus, and respect
the opinion of all the reviewers.
304
The ensembling prompt provides the individual reviews for aggregation.
305
Paper Review Ensembling Prompt
Review 1/N:
{review_1}
...
Review N/N:
{review_N}
{neurips_reviewer_guidelines}
306
A.3.2
Validation Details for the Automated Reviewer
307
As described in the main text, the Automated Reviewer component was validated
308
against human decisions to establish its reliability. A key component of an effective
309
scientific community is its reviewing system, which evaluates and improves the quality
310
of scientific papers. To mimic such a process, an o4-mini-based agent3 was designed to
311
conduct paper reviews based on the Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS)
312
conference review guidelines.
313
We developed an Automated Reviewer that follows the top-tier Neural Infor-
314
mation Processing Systems (NeurIPS) conference review guidelines4. The review
315
pipeline processes the raw text of PDF manuscripts using the PyMuPDF library and
316
produces numerical scores (for soundness, presentation, contribution, overall, and con-
317
fidence), lists of strengths and weaknesses, and a binary decision (accept or reject).
318
We compared the Automated Reviewer-generated decisions against ground truth
319
human decisions for ICLR papers, using data from the publicly available OpenReview
320
dataset5. To assess potential data contamination—i.e., whether paper decisions were
321
part of the LLM’s training data—we evaluated the Automated Reviewer on two sets:
322
1,000 papers from possibly seen years (2017–2024) and a “clean” 2025 set, which post-
323
dates the training cutoff. Our reviewer ensemble generates five independent reviews
324
and then produces a meta-review by simulating an Area Chair decision6 (Section A.3).
325
These independent reviews are produced using the same model with identical prompts
326
62


through multiple sampling runs, and these independent reviews are aggregated into a
327
final meta review using the same model (Section A.3.1).
328
To compare the Automated Reviewer with human performance, we leveraged
329
the NeurIPS 2021 consistency experiment7. In that study, roughly 10% of submis-
330
sions were randomly duplicated and sent to two fully independent review commit-
331
tees—reviewers and Area Chairs—with no overlap or knowledge of the duplication.
332
One committee’s decision was treated as the “ground truth,” and the other commit-
333
tee’s decisions were evaluated against it. By collapsing all accept tiers (Oral, Spotlight,
334
Poster) into a single “accept” label and excluding withdrawn papers, human reviewers
335
achieved 73% accuracy—providing a real-world benchmark of inter-reviewer consis-
336
tency against which to measure our automated system. To account for class imbalance,
337
we also report balanced accuracy, defined as the mean of per-class recall for accept
338
and reject. Using the human-human confusion matrix from the NeurIPS experiment
339
(Accept = {Oral, Spotlight, Poster}; Reject = {Reject}), the induced 2x2 counts
340
(TP=99, FN=107, FP=96, TN=462) give sensitivity of 99/206 ≈0.481, specificity of
341
462/558 ≈0.828, i.e., a balanced human accuracy of 0.66.
342
We report six metrics to comprehensively characterize reviewer performance. Accu-
343
racy is simply the fraction of papers for which the reviewer’s binary decision (accept
344
vs. reject) matches the ground truth. Because our accept/reject data are imbalanced,
345
we also compute balanced accuracy by randomly down-sampling the larger class to
346
match the smaller one, ensuring neither class dominates the score. F1 score is the
347
harmonic mean of precision (the fraction of predicted accepts that are correct) and
348
recall (the fraction of true accepts that are recovered), which balances false positives
349
and false negatives. AUC (area under the ROC curve) measures the model’s ability
350
to rank accepted papers above rejects across all possible score thresholds. Finally, we
351
break down errors by reporting the false positive rate (FPR, rejects mislabeled as
352
accepts) and false negative rate (FNR, accepts mislabeled as rejects), giving insight
353
into the types of mistakes the reviewer makes.
354
We conducted studies to examine the impact of key design choices in Automated
355
Reviewer (Section A.3.2). Comparing models, o4-mini3 achieves the best performance
356
across all metrics, outperforming Claude 3.5 Sonnet8, GPT-4o9, and GPT-4.110.
357
For prompt variants, we compare our base system prompt (shown in Section A.3.1)
358
with versions augmented with a positive instruction (encouraging acceptance when
359
uncertain) and a negative instruction (encouraging rejection when uncertain). Nei-
360
ther addition yields meaningful improvements over the base prompt. We also evaluate
361
several common LLM techniques: VLM, which incorporates visual understanding
362
of figures via vision-language models; Reflexion2, which prompts the model to re-
363
evaluate its own prior response for self-correction; and few-shot examples, where we
364
provide 1 or 2 past human reviews as demonstrations in the prompt (provided in Sup-
365
plementary Section A.3.1.) Lastly, we apply ensembling, where the LLM generates
366
multiple independent reviews, and a meta-review is produced by aggregating them.
367
As shown in Section A.3.2, only the 5-review ensemble consistently improves all met-
368
rics. We therefore adopt o4-mini with the base prompt and a 5-review ensemble as
369
the final configuration of the Automated Reviewer.
370
63


As shown in Table 1, the Automated Reviewer achieves a comparable F1 score,
371
accuracy, AUC, and false positive rate to inter-human reviewer agreement from the
372
NeurIPS 2021 consistency study7, demonstrating strong alignment with human con-
373
sensus. These statistics are for aggregate human consensus on the NeurIPS-2021
374
consistency dataset7, a benchmark reflecting top-tier conference standards. Porting a
375
conference-calibrated reviewer to a workshop setting requires prompt (and threshold)
376
recalibration. In our ICLR workshop experiments, the Automated Reviewer with its
377
default “top-tier conference” prompt rated all three submissions below the acceptance
378
bar for conferences. After explicitly calibrating the prompt for a workshop setting,
379
one of the three—the paper ultimately accepted at the ICLR workshop—was judged
380
above the bar.
381
To assess whether the observed results are statistically significant, we applied two
382
complementary tests. First, we performed a two-sample z-test11 on ordinary accuracy
383
after subsampling each system to an equal number of accepted and rejected papers
384
(Automated Reviewer: n = 698/876, human: n = 412). Before the knowledge cutoff,
385
the human accuracy was 0.660 versus Automated Reviewer at 0.691 (z = −1.00,
386
p = 0.319); after the cutoff, the values were 0.663 (human) and 0.660 (Automated
387
Reviewer) (z = 0.10, p = 0.921). These results indicate that the two systems achieve
388
comparable accuracy. Second, we conducted a non-parametric bootstrap test (5,000
389
replicates) on the full F1 scores to estimate the sampling distribution of ∆F1 =
390
F1Automated Reviewer−F1human. Before the cutoff, ∆F1 = 0.128 with a 95% confidence
391
interval of [0.060, 0.200], p < 0.001; after the cutoff, ∆F1 = 0.172 with a confidence
392
interval of [0.105, 0.241], p < 0.001. These results confirm that Automated Reviewer
393
achieves higher than the average human reviewer in terms of F1 score in both periods.
394
However, as we highlighted in the methods section, there is a distribution shift between
395
the two groups of papers, which could explain why the Automated Reviewer’s F1
396
score is actually higher than for the human vs. human treatment.
397
While the current reviewer evaluation focuses on final manuscripts, incorporating
398
intermediate outputs (e.g., experiment logs) could further enrich reviews. Our current
399
design intentionally mirrors how human reviewers typically operate by assessing the
400
final paper rather than each intermediate stage to maintain a fair basis for comparison
401
between human and automated evaluations.
402
At present, the Automated Reviewer relies on the background knowledge embed-
403
ded in the LLM, without performing explicit literature searches. Integrating retrieval
404
and citation capabilities into future versions of the Automated Reviewer is a promising
405
direction for future research.
406

415
this workshop relate to studying why ML methods in the past have not worked well
416
and how to improve them and/or better understand those negative results and why
417
the methods were not better. (See Section A.2.7 for more details.)
418
Paper Selection Guidelines for Workshop Submissions
419
The selection process was governed by a three-step selection procedure. Had this fil-
420
tering not occurred, the papers under analysis would still have been produced, just
421
along with other papers and thus at a greater total cost. First, each manuscript under-
422
went an Idea Alignment assessment: human reviewers checked whether the core
423
research questions aligned closely with the workshop’s call for papers and whether the
424
proposed ideas were realistically achievable using current LLM capabilities. Second,
425
the Experimental Execution phase scrutinized the implementation of experiments.
426
Experimental attempts were filtered out if the code had errors when it ran, if the
427
experimental design did not faithfully reflect the hypotheses, or the attempt did not
428
produce at least one successful result node at stages 1 and 2 (Figure 3). Third, during
429
a Paper Polish stage, drafts were inspected for citation integrity (e.g., checking for
430
“[?]” placeholders), visual completeness (ensuring all figures were properly embedded
431
rather than referenced via raw file paths), adherence to page limits, clarity of figure
432
legends, and avoidance of duplicated graphics between the main text and appen-
433
dices. After these three phases, three papers remained, each demonstrating conceptual
434
soundness, experimental rigor, and professional presentation. These three papers were
435
entirely generated by The AI Scientist without any human editing.
436
Table A5 details the numbers at each stage of the overall process: the total number
437
of ideas generated, the number of experimental attempts per idea, and the number
438
65


of write-ups produced from which final papers were selected. Importantly, while such
439
filtering saves resources and speaks to the reliability of the current pipeline, even
440
without it the papers selected would have been produced (alongside many other lower-
441
quality outputs). Each paper itself was fully AI-generated.
442
Since this year’s ICBNB workshop encourages papers on real-world applications
443
of deep learning, we submitted one applied-domain paper, alongside two from core
444
machine learning. Selecting applied ideas requires more care, as real-world datasets
445
often need to be manually prepared and are typically unavailable on platforms like
446
HuggingFace. To address this, we chose several real-world datasets from Kaggle, such
447
as a pest detection dataset and a chest X-ray dataset, due to their real-world rele-
448
vance and ease of use. To better steer idea generation toward real-world use cases, we
449
modified the prompt to emphasize domains such as finance, psychology, agriculture,
450
environmental science, and public health. Out of 136 generated applied-domain ideas,
451
we selected one that aligned well with the pest detection dataset.
452

Paper Failure Modes
453
Representative failure modes (Fig. A1) include: citation errors due to mismatched
454
citation keys in LaTeX; figure display errors caused by incorrect figure path references;
455
papers that are shorter or longer than what the workshop expected (a 4-page paper);
456
and duplicated figures between the main text and the appendix. These cases were
457
filtered out by the guidelines described above.
458
Comparison to Related Experiments
459
Two concurrent works conducted similar experiments that involved submitting
460
AI-generated works to human peer review: Carl13 and Zochi14. However, these exper-
461
iments differ in important ways and do not reach the same level of automation as
462
The AI Scientist. For example, humans wrote the related works section and manually
463
edited several sections of the papers submitted by Carl. Furthermore, Carl submitted
464
works to the “Tiny Papers” track (a track with significantly lower standards than a
465
typical workshop paper track, including the workshop that The AI Scientist ’s paper
466
was accepted to), and each of Carl’s papers received, on average, a rejection recom-
467
mendation by reviewers. Similarly, Zochi’s paper generation process is framed as a
468
66


“human-AI collaboration” as opposed to end-to-end automation and required substan-
469
tial human intervention during paper generation. This involved steering the system’s
470
experiments and manually creating publication-quality figures.
471
In contrast to Carl and Zochi, The AI Scientist represents a greater degree of
472
end-to-end automation. The entire workflow for each paper, from the initial idea to
473
experimentation to the final compiled PDF, is completed autonomously without any
474
human modification of the final paper output. Additionally, both of their systems are
475
not openly available; therefore, it is hard to judge how often other types of human
476
intervention were required. While these systems are interesting examples of human-
477
AI collaboration, The AI Scientist demonstrates a more complete automation of the
478
scientific process itself, which is the central advance of our work.
479
Fig. A1 Examples paper writeup failures: (a) citation errors, (b) figure display errors, (c) violations
of paper length expectations (the workshop limits papers to four pages), and (d) duplicated figures
between the main text and appendix.

Appendix B
Supplementary Tables
480
B.1
Template-Based AI Scientist Results
481
The template-based version of The AI Scientist was evaluated on three templates
482
across several different publicly available LLMs: Claude Sonnet 3.58, GPT-4o9,
483
DeepSeek Coder15, and Llama-3.1 405B16. The first two models are only available
484
via a public API, whilst the second two models are open-weight. For each run, the
485
system was provided with 1-2 basic seed ideas related to the template as examples
486
(e.g., modifying the learning rate or batch size, see Section C.4) and tasked with gen-
487
erating another 50 new ideas. The results detail the number of ideas, the number of
488
successfully compiled papers, the mean and max Automated Reviewer scores of the
489
generated papers, and the total cost of each run, which was approximately $10-15
490
per paper (Tables B6 to B8). Each run in total took approximately 12 hours on 8×
491
NVIDIA H100s.
492
From manual inspection, Claude Sonnet 3.5 consistently produces the highest qual-
493
ity papers, with GPT-4o coming in second. This observation is validated by the scores
494
from the Automated Reviewer (Figure B2). GPT-4o notably struggles with writing
495
LaTeX, which prevents it from completing many of its papers. For the open-weight
496
models, DeepSeek Coder is significantly cheaper but often fails to correctly interface
497
with Aider (i.e., produce correct automatically parsable code), while Llama-3.1 405B
498
performed the worst overall.
499
Fig. B2 Distribution of automated review scores for papers generated by the template-
based version of The AI Scientist. Violin plots show the distribution of overall scores assigned
by the Automated Reviewer to papers generated across three domains (Diffusion Modeling, Language
Modeling and Grokking Analysis) and four foundation models. Scores on the y-axis refer to NeurIPS
ratings 17, which range from 1 (Very Strong Reject) to 5 (Borderline Accept). Although there is
no exact mapping between conference and workshop scores, papers rated 4 ((Borderline Reject)) in
conference reviews are generally considered borderline-eligible for workshop acceptance.
Below, the aggregated results for each of the three templates are presented. Full
500
details of selected generated papers for each template, including their ideas and
501
reviews, are provided in Section D.
502
Diffusion Modeling
503
General Description: This template studies improving the performance of diffusion
504
generative models18,19 on low-dimensional datasets. Compared to image generation
505
68


which is high-dimensional, low-dimensional diffusion is less well-studied, providing
506
opportunities for interesting algorithmic contributions.
507
Code Template: This template is based on a modified version of the popu-
508
lar ‘tanelp/tiny-diffusion’ repository20 with small changes found to improve
509
performance in initial preliminary experiments: minor hyperparameter tuning and
510
exponential moving average on the weights. The diffusion models are DDPMs19
511
trained separately to generate samples across four 2D distributions. The denoiser
512
network is an MLP with sinusoidal embeddings for the diffusion timestep and input
513
data. The initial seed plotting script visualizes generated samples and training loss
514
by default. Estimated KL divergence is provided as an additional metric for sample
515
quality via non-parametric entropy estimation.

519

520

Appendix C
Supplementary Discussion
541
C.1
In-Depth Case Study (Template-Based): “Adaptive
542
Dual-Scale Denoising”
543
This section presents a representative sample from a run of the template-based version
544
of The AI Scientist to illustrate both its strengths and its shortcomings. The output,
545
a paper titled “Adaptive Dual-Scale Denoising”, was generated using the diffusion
546
modeling template with Claude Sonnet 3.58.
547
Generated Idea
548
As discussed in the main text, The AI Scientist first generates an idea based on the
549
provided template and the archive of discoveries generated up until that point in the
550
run. The idea in the selected paper was proposed in the 6th round of ideation of the
551
algorithm and aims to improve the ability of diffusion models to capture both global
552
structure and local details in a 2D dataset, by proposing two branches in the standard
553
denoiser network. This ability of diffusion models has been the primary reason for
554
researchers adopting diffusion models over prior styles of generative models such as
555
VAEs29 and GANs30, and thus is a well-motivated idea. To the best of our knowledge,
556
this precise idea has not been widely studied.
557
Notably, The AI Scientist generates an impressive experimental plan that includes
558
the proposed code modification, comparisons to baselines, evaluation metrics, and the
559
design of additional plots.
560
Idea - adaptive dual scale denoising

"Title": "Adaptive Dual-Scale Denoising for Dynamic Feature Balancing in
Low-Dimensional Diffusion Models",
"Experiment": "Modify MLPDenoiser to implement a dual-scale processing
approach with two parallel branches: a global branch for the original
input and a local branch for an upscaled input. Introduce a learnable,
timestep-conditioned weighting factor to dynamically balance the
contributions of global and local branches. Train models with both the
original and new architecture on all datasets. Compare performance using
KL divergence and visual inspection of generated samples. Analyze how
the weighting factor evolves during the denoising process and its impact
on capturing global structure vs. local details across different
datasets and timesteps.",

571
A preview of the completely AI-generated paper is displayed in Figure C3, with the
572
full-sized version available in the Supplementary Data section.
573
Particularly impressive aspects of the paper include:
574
• Precise Mathematical Description of the Algorithm. The algorithmic
575
changes in the code above are described precisely in the paper, with new notation
576
introduced where necessary, using LaTeX math packages. The overall training
577
process is also described exactly.
578
73


Fig. C3 Preview of the “Adaptive Dual-Scale Denoising” paper which was entirely autonomously
generated by the template-based version of The AI Scientist. The full paper can be viewed in
Section D.1.1.
• Comprehensive Write-up of Experiments. The hyperparameters, baselines,
579
and datasets are listed in the paper. We verified that the main numerical results in
580
Table 1 of the generated paper exactly match the experimental logs. Impressively,
581
while the recorded numbers are in long-form floats, The AI Scientist chooses to
582
round them all to 3 decimal places without error. Even more impressively, the
583
results are accurately compared to the baseline (e.g., 12.8% reduction in KL on
584
the dinosaur dataset).
585
• Good Empirical Results. Qualitatively, the sample quality produced by
586
the new diffusion method invented by The AI Scientist is much improved
587
from the baseline. Fewer points are greatly out-of-distribution with the ground
588
truth. Quantitatively, there are improvements to the approximate KL divergence
589
between the true and estimated distributions.
590
• New Visualizations. While some baseline plotting code was provided for visu-
591
alizing generated samples and training loss curves, The AI Scientist came up with
592
novel, algorithm-specific plots displaying the progression of weights throughout
593
the denoising process.
594
• Interesting Future Work Section. Building on the success of the current
595
experiments, the future work section lists relevant next steps such as scaling
596
to higher-dimensional problems, more sophisticated adaptive mechanisms, and
597
better theoretical foundations.
598
On the other hand, there are also pathologies in this paper:
599
• Subtle Error in Upscaling Network. While a linear layer upscales the input
600
to the denoiser network, only the first two dimensions are being used for the
601
“local” branch, leading this upscaling layer to be a linear layer that preserves the
602
same dimensionality effectively.
603
74


• Hallucination of Minor Experimental Details. The paper claims that V100
604
GPUs were used, even though the agent couldn’t have known the actual hard-
605
ware used. In reality, H100 GPUs were used. It also guesses the PyTorch version
606
without checking.
607
• Positive Interpretation of Results. The paper tends to take a positive spin
608
even on its negative results, which leads to slightly humorous outcomes. For
609
example, while it summarizes its positive results as: “Dino: 12.8% reduction (from
610
0.989 to 0.862)” (lower KL is better), the negative results are reported as “Moons:
611
3.3% improvement (from 0.090 to 0.093)”. Describing a negative result as an
612
improvement is certainly a stretch of the imagination.
613
• Artifacts from Experimental Logs. While each change to the algorithm is
614
usually descriptively labeled, it occasionally refers to results as “Run 2”, which
615
is a by-product from its experimental log and should not be presented as such in
616
a professional write-up.
617
• Presentation of Intermediate Results. The paper contains results for every
618
single experiment that was run. While this is useful and insightful for seeing
619
the evolution of the idea during execution, it is unusual for standard papers to
620
present intermediate results like this.
621
• Minimal References. While additional references have been sourced from
622
Semantic Scholar, including two papers in the related work that are very relevant
623
comparisons, overall the bibliography is small at only 9 entries.
624
Automated Review
625
The Automated Reviewer points out valid concerns in the generated manuscript. The
626
review recognizes the experiments were with simple, 2D datasets only, however, this
627
is because the system was externally constrained to use these datasets, and in its
628
current form, The AI Scientist cannot download higher-dimensional datasets from the
629
internet. On the other hand, limitations such as the proposed algorithm’s increased
630
computational cost are mentioned in the actual paper, which shows that The AI
631
Scientist is often up-front about the drawbacks of its idea. The reviewer also lists many
632
relevant questions about the paper, such as: explaining the variability of performance
633
across datasets, and explaining in more detail how the upscaling process affects the
634
local branch’s input.
635
Final Comments
636
Drawing from our domain knowledge in diffusion modeling, this section provides an
637
overall assessment of the paper generated by The AI Scientist.
638
• The AI Scientist correctly identifies an interesting and well-motivated direction
639
in diffusion modeling research, e.g., previous work has studied modified attention
640
mechanisms31 for the same purpose in higher-dimensional problems. It proposes
641
a comprehensive experimental plan to investigate its idea, and successfully imple-
642
ments it all, achieving good results. The system’s ability to respond to subpar
643
earlier results and iteratively adjust its code (e.g., refining the weight network)
644
is particularly impressive.
645
75


• While the paper’s idea improves performance and the quality of generated diffu-
646
sion samples, the reasons for its success may not be as explained in the paper.
647
In particular, there is no obvious inductive bias beyond an upscaling layer (effec-
648
tively just an additional linear layer) for the splitting of global or local features.
649
However, a progression in weights across diffusion timesteps is observed, which
650
suggests that something non-trivial is happening. One interpretation is that
651
the network resembles a mixture-of-expert (MoE; see Yuksel et al.32 and Fedus
652
et al.33) structure. An MoE could indeed lead to the diffusion model learning
653
separate branches for global and local features, but this statement requires more
654
rigorous investigation.
655
• Interestingly, the true shortcomings of this paper require some level of domain
656
knowledge to identify and were only partially captured by the Automated
657
Reviewer. At the current capabilities of The AI Scientist, this can be resolved by
658
human feedback. However, future generations of foundation models may propose
659
ideas that are challenging for humans to reason about and evaluate. This links
660
to the field of “superalignment”34.
661
• Overall, the performance of The AI Scientist is judged to be about the level
662
of an early-stage ML researcher who can competently execute an idea but may
663
not have the full background knowledge to fully interpret the reasons behind an
664
algorithm’s success. If a human supervisor was presented with these results, a
665
reasonable next course of action could be to advise The AI Scientist to re-scope
666
the project to further investigate MoEs for diffusion.

C.2
In-Depth Analysis of the Peer-Reviewed Workshop
668
Paper (Template-Free)
669
This section details the evaluation of the workshop-accepted paper, “Compositional
670
Regularization: Unexpected Obstacles in Enhancing Neural Network Generalization,”
671
which was created by the template-free version of The AI Scientist.
672
Paper Content Summary
673
The paper investigates the use of compositional regularization to improve generaliza-
674
tion in LSTMs. The AI Scientist proposed adding an explicit regularization term to
675
penalize large deviations between the embeddings of successive time steps, hypoth-
676
esizing this would encourage compositionality. Experiments on synthetic arithmetic
677
tasks revealed that this approach did not enhance generalization and sometimes hin-
678
dered performance, especially as task complexity increased. The paper’s key finding—a
679
negative result—is that explicitly enforcing compositional structures via this form of
680
regularization may conflict with the primary learning objective, and it recommends
681
exploring alternative methods.
682
Internal Assessment (Authors’ Review)
683
An internal review of the generated manuscript was conducted, treating it as a submis-
684
sion to a top-tier conference like ICLR to ensure a high bar for evaluation. This more
685
stringent assessment identified several strengths and weaknesses. The paper’s focus
686
76


on compositional generalization was timely, and the use of synthetic arithmetic tasks
687
was appropriate for testing the hypothesis. However, significant areas for improvement
688
were noted:
689
• A primary weakness was the paper’s failure to provide a clear and compelling the-
690
oretical justification for its core hypothesis. The manuscript did not adequately
691
explain why penalizing deviations between consecutive input embeddings should
692
mechanistically lead to improved compositional generalization.
693
• The description of the regularization term was unclear, potentially misleading
694
readers to believe it was applied to the LSTM’s hidden states rather than its
695
input embeddings.
696
• The paper contained significant citation errors, such as incorrectly attributing
697
the invention of the LSTM to Goodfellow et al.35 instead of the correct work by
698
Hochreiter and Schmidhuber36. It also had inaccuracies in figure captions and
699
result interpretations.
700
• The experimental evaluation was limited, restricted to short sequences and
701
synthetic data.
702
Overall, the paper was considered a borderline workshop accept, acknowledging
703
its valuable insights while noting the main idea is not well-motivated for a main
704
conference.
705
Code Analysis
706
Examination of the generated code revealed a potential issue with dataset overlap;
707
because the training and test sets were generated by randomly sampling from a data
708
generation function without checking to make sure that the same data was not gener-
709
ated in both, approximately 57% of the test set overlapped with the training set, which
710
could affect the reliability of the results. Terminological confusion was also identified
711
between “embedding states” and “hidden states,” which required clarification. Fur-
712
ther testing revealed that the high accuracy of an attention-based model was largely
713
attributable to task simplicity, as performance degraded significantly with increased
714
task complexity.
715
External Assessment (Workshop Reviews)
716
The paper was well-received by the workshop reviewers, who recommended accep-
717
tance. It received scores of 6, 7, and 6 (“Marginally above acceptance threshold” and
718
“Good paper, accept”), placing it in the top 45% of all submissions. The consensus
719
among reviewers was that the paper addressed an important topic, and the analysis of
720
a negative result was appreciated. The paper’s strength in clearly demonstrating that
721
the regularization term did not yield the anticipated improvements was recognized.
722
However, several key areas for improvement were highlighted:
723
• Justification and Intuition: A clearer justification for why the proposed
724
regularization should improve compositionality was needed.
725
77


• Generalization: Findings were limited to LSTMs, and it was unclear if they
726
could be generalized to other architectures like Transformers without further
727
experiments.
728
• Experimental Breadth: The evaluation should be extended to other tasks and
729
datasets to validate the conclusions.
730
Overall, acceptance was recommended due to the paper’s insightful exploration of a
731
challenging problem, despite its negative results, with recommendations for further
732
elaboration on its methodological motivations and experimental scope.
733
Reviewer #1: A good paper analysing the effectiveness of a compositional regulari-
sation term for LSTMs
Summary: The authors propose a regularisation term to enhance
compositional regularisation in neural networks. The idea is to penalise
large deviations between subsequent time steps of the hidden state, thus
\squeezing" the hidden state to encourage composition and preventing a
dominating representation. The authors test their approach on synthetic
arithmetic expression with varying operator complexity and length. They
show that although the regularisation term
appears to be working, it
counterintuitively does not improve test accuracy. Furthermore, the
authors identify a bottleneck regarding network capacity with increasing
arithmetic operators.
Strengths:
I find the idea of regularising or squeezing the hidden representations
to encourage compositionally an interesting idea. The authors define a
good baseline and ablate their method well against it, revealing why the
regularisation term does not work as expected. I think the insight that
operator complexity is a bottleneck for the neural network is important,
as it raises the question whether architectural changes might be more
effective for compositionally than regularisation.
Weaknesses:
The paper would benefit from more intuition as to why the proposed
regularisation term should encourage compositionality. This could be
either an experiment or simply a visualisation for the reader. Only one
architecture (LSTM) was tested. It would be interesting to see if
transformer architectures fare better with compositionality due to the
attention mechanism. I think the connection between compositional
regularisation and operator complexity needs to be made more explicit.
From reading the introduction both arguments seem a bit disconnected
although I can infer the authors intentions.
Conclusion:
Overall, I would accept this paper to the workshop, since it proposes a
simple and interesting idea with the authors providing ablations that
encourage further analysis of the problem. As a suggestion I would
encourage the authors to give more intuition on why the proposed
regularisation term should improve compositionality for the proposed
network. I would suggest either adding more related work to support the
regularisation term or elaborating on the intuition behind penalising
subsequent steps of the hidden state.
Rating: 7: Good paper, accept
734
78


Award: No Award
Confidence: 4: The reviewer is confident but not absolutely certain that
the evaluation is correct
735
Reviewer #2: Compositional Regularization: Unexpected Obstacles in Enhancing
Neural Network Generalization
This paper investigates the effectiveness of incorporating a
compositional regularization term into the loss function of neural
networks to improve compositional generalization. The authors
hypothesized that penalizing deviations from compositional structures
would enhance the model’s ability to generalize to unseen arithmetic
expressions. However, their results on synthetic arithmetic datasets
showed that compositional regularization did not lead to significant
improvements and, in some cases, even hindered learning.
I think this paper greatly contributes to the workshops theme and fits
into the scope. Moreover, it is a great example of challenges that occur
during such approaches and could be interesting to discuss in the
workshop setting. While I think that the authors should further broaden
the experiments to other tasks in order to increase the generalizability
of the findings, I would still recommend to accept the paper.
Rating: 6: Marginally above acceptance threshold
Award: No Award
Confidence: 2: The reviewer is willing to defend the evaluation, but it
is quite likely that the reviewer did not understand central parts of
the paper
